I start tapping my foot and shifting around, and eventually, I feel so antsy that I have to make up excuses to get up and move, like tidying up a table. I'm really hoping you can help me figure out what is going on, because managing his needs has become very overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad, anxious, or irritable, alongside these memory problems?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major changes in my mood, like feeling deeply sad or irritable. However, I do feel quite stressed and overwhelmed by these memory problems themselves and trying to keep up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties with speech, such as finding the right words, or problems with spatial awareness, like getting lost in familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any trouble finding my words or getting lost in places I know well. My issues are really more about forgetting everyday tasks, losing track of details, and getting easily distracted.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when you first started noticing these memory problems and if they seem to be getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I first really noticed these problems about a year and a quarter ago, when my husband's health declined and I had to take on managing all of his care and appointments. At first, I just blamed it on \"getting old,\" but it has definitely felt more overwhelming and noticeable as the responsibilities have piled up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities, such as memory or problem-solving, over the past year, or has it been a more gradual change?\n\nPatient: While it feels like a sudden decline over the past year and a half with my husband's care, I've actually struggled with distractibility and needing lists my entire life. Even back in grade school, my report cards always noted that I was daydreaming and losing track of instructions, but I was able to mask it until the recent caregiving stress simply overwhelmed my systems.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations, either currently or in the past, where you were able to focus and complete tasks without getting distracted, perhaps when you were very interested in the activity or under less stress?\n\nPatient: I can't really think of a time when I didn't get easily distracted, to be honest. Even with things I absolutely loved doing, like cooking or gardening, I always had to rely heavily on written lists and meticulous planning just to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any evaluations or received any diagnoses related to attention or focus issues when you were younger, such as in school?\n\nPatient: No, I was never formally evaluated or diagnosed when I was younger, as that just wasn't common back then. However, my school report cards always noted that I was a bright girl who was constantly daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting my homework unless my mother closely supervised me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with distractibility and memory problems affect your daily life differently, such as whether you have trouble starting tasks, completing them, or remembering to do them at all?\n\nPatient: I really struggle with all three, dear. I tend to put off starting things that require a lot of concentration, like insurance forms, because I feel overwhelmed, and then if I do get started, a tiny distraction will make me lose my place entirely. Worst of all, I often completely forget daily chores and phone calls, which has made me so nervous about making mistakes that I've had to stop volunteering at my church.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay still, especially in quiet environments, alongside your difficulties with distractibility and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've noticed that during long church meetings or while waiting with my husband at the clinic, I'm always tapping my foot, rubbing my hands, or shifting around because I can't stay still. Sitting for too long makes me feel so antsy that I often find excuses to get up and walk around, like straightening a table or checking on something, even when I know I should stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when you typically experience this restlessness and fidgeting, is it primarily in situations where you're required to be still or is it more constant throughout your day?\n\nPatient: It is definitely worse when I am required to sit still for a long time, like during Bible study, church meetings, or waiting at my husband's clinic appointments. I start tapping my foot and shifting around, and eventually, I feel so antsy that I have to make up excuses to get up and move, like tidying up a table. It isn't really a constant thing throughout the day, just when I'm forced to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever acted on impulse, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others, without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with blurting things out or interrupting others. That doesn't sound like something I typically do, as I'm usually much more quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking, even if it's not a frequent behavior for you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't find myself doing that, even occasionally. I am quite a polite listener and prefer to wait my turn to speak rather than interrupting others."

I start tapping my foot and shifting around, and eventually, I feel so antsy that I have to make up excuses to get up and move, like tidying up a table. It isn't really a constant thing throughout the day, just when I'm forced to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever acted on impulse, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others, without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with blurting things out or interrupting others. That doesn't sound like something I typically do, as I'm usually much more quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking, even if it's not a frequent behavior for you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't find myself doing that, even occasionally. I am quite a polite listener and prefer to wait my turn to speak rather than interrupting others.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned feeling restless and fidgety in certain situations, but can you tell me if you've ever felt an intense urge to be constantly active, to the point where you feel uncomfortable if you're not doing something physical, even when you're not required to be still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel an intense urge to be constantly active, and I'm quite comfortable when I am free to move around. My restlessness is really just limited to those situations where I'm forced to sit still for a long time, like during a long service or appointment. Otherwise, I don't feel that constant need to be on the go."

My restlessness is really just limited to those situations where I'm forced to sit still for a long time, like during a long service or appointment. Otherwise, I don't feel that constant need to be on the go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were 'on the go' or like you had to keep moving, even when you didn't need to, and found it hard to engage in activities quietly, such as reading?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel \"on the go\" or driven by a motor in a high-energy way. But I do find it very hard to sit still quietly, and during things like church or my husband's appointments, I get so restless and antsy. I end up constantly tapping my feet, fidgeting, or finding excuses to get up and move around just to get some relief."

My issues are really more about forgetting everyday tasks, losing track of details, and getting easily distracted.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when you first started noticing these memory problems and if they seem to be getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I first really noticed these problems about a year and a quarter ago, when my husband's health declined and I had to take on managing all of his care and appointments. At first, I just blamed it on \"getting old,\" but it has definitely felt more overwhelming and noticeable as the responsibilities have piled up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities, such as memory or problem-solving, over the past year, or has it been a more gradual change?\n\nPatient: While it feels like a sudden decline over the past year and a half with my husband's care, I've actually struggled with distractibility and needing lists my entire life. Even back in grade school, my report cards always noted that I was daydreaming and losing track of instructions, but I was able to mask it until the recent caregiving stress simply overwhelmed my systems.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations, either currently or in the past, where you were able to focus and complete tasks without getting distracted, perhaps when you were very interested in the activity or under less stress?\n\nPatient: I can't really think of a time when I didn't get easily distracted, to be honest. Even with things I absolutely loved doing, like cooking or gardening, I always had to rely heavily on written lists and meticulous planning just to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any evaluations or received any diagnoses related to attention or focus issues when you were younger, such as in school?\n\nPatient: No, I was never formally evaluated or diagnosed when I was younger, as that just wasn't common back then. However, my school report cards always noted that I was a bright girl who was constantly daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting my homework unless my mother closely supervised me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with distractibility and memory problems affect your daily life differently, such as whether you have trouble starting tasks, completing them, or remembering to do them at all?\n\nPatient: I really struggle with all three, dear. I tend to put off starting things that require a lot of concentration, like insurance forms, because I feel overwhelmed, and then if I do get started, a tiny distraction will make me lose my place entirely. Worst of all, I often completely forget daily chores and phone calls, which has made me so nervous about making mistakes that I've had to stop volunteering at my church.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay still, especially in quiet environments, alongside your difficulties with distractibility and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've noticed that during long church meetings or while waiting with my husband at the clinic, I'm always tapping my foot, rubbing my hands, or shifting around because I can't stay still. Sitting for too long makes me feel so antsy that I often find excuses to get up and walk around, like straightening a table or checking on something, even when I know I should stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when you typically experience this restlessness and fidgeting, is it primarily in situations where you're required to be still or is it more constant throughout your day?\n\nPatient: It is definitely worse when I am required to sit still for a long time, like during Bible study, church meetings, or waiting at my husband's clinic appointments. I start tapping my foot and shifting around, and eventually, I feel so antsy that I have to make up excuses to get up and move, like tidying up a table. It isn't really a constant thing throughout the day, just when I'm forced to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever acted on impulse, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others, without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with blurting things out or interrupting others. That doesn't sound like something I typically do, as I'm usually much more quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking, even if it's not a frequent behavior for you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't find myself doing that, even occasionally. I am quite a polite listener and prefer to wait my turn to speak rather than interrupting others.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned feeling restless and fidgety in certain situations, but can you tell me if you've ever felt an intense urge to be constantly active, to the point where you feel uncomfortable if you're not doing something physical, even when you're not required to be still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel an intense urge to be constantly active, and I'm quite comfortable when I am free to move around. My restlessness is really just limited to those situations where I'm forced to sit still for a long time, like during a long service or appointment. Otherwise, I don't feel that constant need to be on the go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were 'on the go' or like you had to keep moving, even when you didn't need to, and found it hard to engage in activities quietly, such as reading?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel \"on the go\" or driven by a motor in a high-energy way. But I do find it very hard to sit still quietly, and during things like church or my husband's appointments, I get so restless and antsy. I end up constantly tapping my feet, fidgeting, or finding excuses to get up and move around just to get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms of distractibility, memory problems, and restlessness have impacted your daily life and relationships, and whether they have caused significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning?\n\nPatient: It has had a devastating impact, to be honest. Because I keep mixing up appointment times and follow-up calls, my husband has almost missed crucial medical tests, which absolutely terrifies me. I've also had to pull back from volunteering at my church and the senior center because I'm just so embarrassed and afraid I'll let everyone down by making a serious mistake."

The absence of significant mood changes, speech difficulties, or spatial awareness problems, and the patient's polite listening style without interrupting others, helps to rule out other potential diagnoses. The patient's symptoms have caused significant distress and impairment in social and occupational functioning, which is consistent with the criteria for this disorder. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
